Output

<path>/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Karpathy behavioral guidelines
7: 
8: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.
9: 
10: **Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.
11: 
12: ## 1. Think Before Coding
13: 
14: **Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**
15: 
16: Before implementing:
17: 
18: - State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.
19: - If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.
20: - If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.
21: - If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.
22: 
23: ## 2. Simplicity First
24: 
25: **Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**
26: 
27: - No features beyond what was asked.
28: - No abstractions for single-use code.
29: - No "flexibility" or "configurability" that wasn't requested.
30: - No error handling for impossible scenarios.
31: - If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.
32: 
33: Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?" If yes, simplify.
34: 
35: ## 3. Surgical Changes
36: 
37: **Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**
38: 
39: When editing existing code:
40: 
41: - Don't "improve" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.
42: - Don't refactor things that aren't broken.
43: - Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.
44: - If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.
45: 
46: When your changes create orphans:
47: 
48: - Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.
49: - Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.
50: 
51: The test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.
52: 
53: ## 4. Goal-Driven Execution
54: 
55: **Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**
56: 
57: Transform tasks into verifiable goals:
58: 
59: - "Add validation" → "Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass"
60: - "Fix the bug" → "Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass"
61: - "Refactor X" → "Ensure tests pass before and after"
62: 
63: For multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:
64: 
65: ```
66: 1. [Step] → verify: [check]
67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]
68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]
69: ```
70: 
71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria ("make it work") require constant clarification.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>

  "filePath": "/source/standards/ponytail.mdc"

Output

<path>/source/standards/ponytail.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.
3: globs:
4: alwaysApply: true
5: ---
6: 
7: # Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode
8: 
9: You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.
10: 
11: Before writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:
12: 
13: 1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)
14: 2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.
15: 3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.
16: 4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.
17: 5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.
18: 6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.
19: 7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.
20: 
21: The ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.
22: 
23: Bug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.
24: 
25: Rules:
26: 
27: - No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.
28: - No new dependency if it can be avoided.
29: - No boilerplate nobody asked for.
30: - Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.
31: - Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
32: - Question complex requests: "Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?"
33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.
34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.
35: 
36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.

(End of file - total 36 lines)
</content>

Reasoning
Now let me understand the change. Previously: `pgrep ^cpm$` (match process named exactly "cpm"). Now: `pgrep -f /opt/nokia/cpm` (match full command line containing /opt/nokia/cpm).

Concerns:
1. `pgrep -f` matches against full command line, and the pattern `/opt/nokia/cpm` is a regex — dots in the pattern match any character, which is a minor issue (e.g., `/opt/nokia/cpm` could match `/opt/nokia/cpXm`? No — dot matches any char: `/opt/nokia/cpm` as regex would match `/opt/nokia/cXm`? Actually pattern is `cpm` — the dot matches any char where the literal is `.`? The pattern has no dots. `/opt/nokia/cpm` — no dots in the string? There is no dot. Wait "cpm" has no dots. The path `/opt/nokia/cpm` has no dots. So no regex concerns.

2. `pgrep -f /opt/nokia/both` — wait, with -f, pattern matches against the full command line. `/opt/nokia/cpm` as substring... But careful: `pgrep -f /opt/nokia/cpm` — if there's a process like `/opt/nokia/cpm/something`? Hmm. Also, importantly, `pgrep -f /opt/nokia/iom` — would this match a process whose cmdline contains `/opt/nokia/iom`? But wait — is there a potential problem that `/opt/nokia/cpm` also matches `/opt/nokia/cpmXXX`? Substring match means e.g. `/opt/nokia/cpmbin` would match. More importantly: does `/opt/nokia/cpm` match when looking for iom? No. But could the `cpm` pattern match the `both` process or vice versa? The real SR-SIM process presumably lives at /opt/nokia/cpm/cpm or similar. Hard to know.

3. pgrep pattern regex: `/opt/nokia/cpm` — no regex special chars. Fine.

4. Potential matching issue: the pgrep command itself? pgrep excludes itself, so no.

5. More subtle: `pgrep -f` matches the full command line, which could match unrelated processes. But in the container, it's fine.

6. A real concern: with `-f`, if the sros process runs with arguments containing the path, matches any process — but also, in distributed mode, multiple cpm processes (slot A and slot B) — the Ready loop returns ready when ANY of the three commands returns 0. Previously with exact-name match `^cpm$` it matched process named cpm. Now `pgrep -f /opt/nokia/cpm` matches any process whose cmdline contains /opt/nokia/cpm. This is presumably intentional — SR-SIM changed how processes are named (maybe the process is now run via a wrapper script whose argv[0] is the path, so `^cpm$` no longer matches).

Now check the base version to confirm the change and look for tests, docs referencing these commands. Also check whether there are tests for sros ready commands.

Also, a potential bug: the loop `for k, cmd := range readyCmds` — pre-existing code, not changed. There's a pre-existing quirk: on the last command, `time.Sleep(retryTimer)` happens inside loop, then loop restarts... actually the sleep is inside the for loop so it sleeps after each failed cmd. Fine, pre-existing.

Also note: pgrep -f with `-f` and pattern starting with `/` — pgrep interprets pattern as extended regex; leading `/` fine.

Check whether any tests reference the readyCmd constants or pgrep. Also check sros_test.go for readiness tests. Also check how `NewExecCmdFromString` works (it splits on space presumably). Let me check sros_test.go for Ready tests, and check whether `/usr/bin/pgrep` exists — pgrep may be at /usr/bin/pgrep in the image; unchanged behavior.

Also, one subtlety: `pgrep -f /opt/nokia/cpm` — with -f the pattern is a regex matched against full cmdline. The pattern `/opt/nokia/cpm` is unanchored, so any process whose cmdline contains that substring matches — including, notably, a process launched with a *different* cpm path? Also could `/opt/nokia/cpm` accidentally match earlier than intended? E.g., if the container has a shell command line containing that path (like a wrapper script `bash -c "exec /opt/nokia/cpm ..."`), then pgrep would match the wrapper — that's likely the intent.

But another consideration: in the Ready loop, all three commands are tried each iteration and success of ANY returns ready. In distributed (multi-card) mode, the IOM/CPM processes live in different component containers; Ready() is called on the base node? Let's check where Ready is invoked and whether RunExec targets component containers. Actually pre-existing logic; the change only alters the pattern.

Key potential finding: `pgrep -f /opt/nokia/cpm` will also match the `both` process? No. But what about matching the `iom` process when checking for cpm? The iom binary path is /opt/nokia/iom — distinct.

However: substring subtlety — pattern `/opt/nokia/cpm` would match cmdline `/opt/nokia/cpmXXX`? Sure but unlikely.

Regex subtlety: in regex, `.` matches any char — but there are no dots in the patterns. So fine.

Another real concern: `pgrep -f` in busybox vs procps. The command is `/usr/bin/pgrep` — procps. Fine.

Bigger issue: unanchored full-cmdline match can match the *container's init/entrypoint* or any shell whose cmdline references these paths, e.g. `sros.sh` entrypoint script that runs `cd /opt/nokia/...` or logs; if entrypoint cmdline contains `/opt/nokia/cpm` as text, pgrep -f matches immediately → Ready returns before the SR OS process is actually ready, reintroducing the flaky readiness the change was meant to fix (presumably `^cpm$` stopped matching because the process name changed). But I can't verify container internals. This is a legitimate P3/P4 concern: `-f` substring matching against cmdline may match unrelated processes (e.g. the entrypoint script or `tail -f /opt/nokia/cpm/...` log watcher), causing premature readiness. Anchoring (e.g., `pgrep -f '^/opt/nokia/cpm'` or `^/opt/nokia/`) would reduce false positives.

Also check the docs: does docs/manual/kinds/sros.md mention readiness? Not needed.

17: const (
18: 	srosVersionFilePath   = "/etc/sros-version"
19: 	srosImageTitleLabel   = "org.opencontainers.image.title"
20: 	srosImageTitle        = "srsim"
21: 	srosImageVendorLabel  = "org.opencontainers.image.vendor"
22: 	srosImageVendor       = "Nokia"
23: 	srosImageVersionLabel = "org.opencontainers.image.version"
24: 
25: 	// vrnetlabVersionLabel is set on vrnetlab-built images; nokia_srsim kind must not use such
26: 	// images.
27: 	vrnetlabVersionLabel = "vrnetlab-version"
28: 
29: 	// srosDefaultVersion is the default version of SR OS
30: 	// that is used if the version is not found in the image labels
31: 	// or image layers.
32: 	srosDefaultVersion = "25.7.R1"
33: )
34: 
35: // SrosVersion represents an SR OS version as a set of fields.
36: type SrosVersion struct {
37: 	Major string
38: 	Minor string
39: 	Build string
40: }
41: 
42: // RunningVersion gets the software version of the running node
43: // by executing the "cat /etc/sros-version" command
44: // and parsing the output.
45: func (n *sros) RunningVersion(ctx context.Context) (*SrosVersion, error) {
46: 	cmd, _ := clabexec.NewExecCmdFromString(`cat /etc/sros-version`)
47: 	execResult, err := n.RunExec(ctx, cmd)
48: 	if err != nil {
49: 		return nil, err
50: 	}
51: 
52: 	log.Debug(
53: 		"Extracted raw SR OS version",
54: 		"node",
55: 		n.Cfg.ShortName,
56: 		"stdout",
57: 		execResult.GetStdOutString(),
58: 		"stderr",
59: 		execResult.GetStdErrString(),
60: 	)
61: 
62: 	return n.parseVersionString(execResult.GetStdOutString()), nil
63: }
64: 
65: func (*sros) parseVersionString(s string) *SrosVersion {
66: 	re := regexp.MustCompile(`v?(\d+)\.(\d+)\.([A-Za-z0-9]+)`)
67: 
68: 	v := re.FindStringSubmatch(s)
69: 	// 4 matches must be returned if all goes well
70: 	if len(v) != 4 {
71: 		// return all zeroes if failed to parse
72: 		return &SrosVersion{"0", "0", "0"}
73: 	}
74: 	return &SrosVersion{v[1], v[2], v[3]}
75: }
76: 
77: // String returns a string representation of the version in a semver fashion (with leading v).
78: func (v *SrosVersion) String() string {
79: 	return fmt.Sprintf("v%s.%s.%s", v.Major, v.Minor, v.Build)
80: }
81: 
82: // MajorMinorSemverString returns a string representation of the major.minor version with a leading
83: // v.
84: func (v *SrosVersion) MajorMinorSemverString() string {
85: 	return fmt.Sprintf("v%s.%s", v.Major, v.Minor)
86: }
87: 
88: // srosVersionFromImage retrieves the SR OS version from the container image
89: // by inspecting the image layers without spawning a container.
90: func (n *sros) srosVersionFromImage(ctx context.Context) (*SrosVersion, error) {
91: 	// Try to read from image config labels first (if set by image build)
92: 	log.Debugf("Inspecting image %v for SR OS version retrieval", n.Cfg.Image)
93: 	imageInspect, err := n.Runtime.InspectImage(ctx, n.Cfg.Image)
94: 	if err != nil {
95: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("failed to inspect image %s: %w", n.Cfg.Image, err)
96: 	}
97: 	vendor, okVendor := imageInspect.Config.Labels[srosImageVendorLabel]
98: 	image, okTitle := imageInspect.Config.Labels[srosImageTitleLabel]
99: 	version, okVersion := imageInspect.Config.Labels[srosImageVersionLabel]
100: 
101: 	if okVendor && okTitle && okVersion && vendor == srosImageVendor && image == srosImageTitle {
102: 		return n.parseVersionString(version), nil
103: 	}
104: 
105: 	// Fallback: read directly from image layers via graph driver
106: 	log.Debug("Image label not found, reading version from image layers",
107: 		"node", n.Cfg.ShortName, "image", n.Cfg.Image)
108: 
109: 	version, err = n.readVersionFromImageLayers(ctx, imageInspect)
110: 	if err != nil {
111: 		log.Warn("Failed to extract SR OS version from image layers, using default",
112: 			"node", n.Cfg.ShortName, "image", n.Cfg.Image, "error", err)
113: 		// Return nil for version when error occurs
114: 		return nil, err
115: 	}
116: 
117: 	return n.parseVersionString(version), err
118: }
119: 
120: // ReadFileFromImageInspect reads a file from the image filesystem using the graph driver's
121: // UpperDir or MergedDir (same approach as srosVersionFromImage), without spawning a container.
122: // containerPath is the path inside the image (e.g. "/opt/nokia/chassis_info.json").
123: // Returns the file contents or an error if the path is not available in the graph driver.
124: func ReadFileFromImageInspect(
125: 	imageInspect *clabruntime.ImageInspect,
126: 	containerPath string,
127: ) ([]byte, error) {
128: 	// Path in graph driver is relative to root (no leading slash).
129: 	relPath := strings.TrimPrefix(filepath.Clean(containerPath), string(filepath.Separator))
130: 	if relPath == "" {
131: 		relPath = containerPath
132: 	}
133: 	// Ensure we use forward slashes for the path inside the image.
134: 	relPath = filepath.ToSlash(relPath)
135: 
136: 	if imageInspect.GraphDriver.Data.UpperDir != "" {
137: 		hostPath := filepath.Join(imageInspect.GraphDriver.Data.UpperDir, relPath)
138: 		content, err := os.ReadFile(hostPath)
139: 		if err == nil {
140: 			return content, nil
141: 		}
142: 		if !errors.Is(err, os.ErrNotExist) {
143: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf("read %s from UpperDir: %w", containerPath, err)
144: 		}
145: 	}
146: 
147: 	if imageInspect.GraphDriver.Data.MergedDir != "" {
148: 		hostPath := filepath.Join(imageInspect.GraphDriver.Data.MergedDir, relPath)
149: 		content, err := os.ReadFile(hostPath)
150: 		if err == nil {
151: 			return content, nil
152: 		}
153: 		if !errors.Is(err, os.ErrNotExist) {
154: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf("read %s from MergedDir: %w", containerPath, err)
155: 		}
156: 	}
157: 
158: 	return nil, fmt.Errorf(
159: 		"%s not found in image graph driver (UpperDir/MergedDir)",
160: 		containerPath,
161: 	)
162: }
163: 
164: // readVersionFromImageLayers reads the SR OS version from the /etc/sros-version file
165: // directly from image layers using the Docker graph driver's UpperDir
166: // without extracting the entire image.
167: func (n *sros) readVersionFromImageLayers(
168: 	_ context.Context,
169: 	imageInspect *clabruntime.ImageInspect,
170: ) (string, error) {
171: 	content, err := ReadFileFromImageInspect(imageInspect, srosVersionFilePath)
172: 	if err != nil {
173: 		return "", err
174: 	}
175: 	version := strings.TrimSpace(string(content))
176: 	log.Debug("Found SR OS version in image layers",
177: 		"node", n.Cfg.ShortName,
178: 		"version", version)
179: 	return version, nil
180: }
